=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an array of integers nums. Some values in nums are missing and are denoted by -1.
You can choose a pair of positive integers (x, y) exactly once and replace each missing element with either x or y.
You need to minimize the maximum absolute difference between adjacent elements of nums after replacements.
Return the minimum possible difference.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [1,2,-1,10,8]
Output: 4
Explanation:
By choosing the pair as (6, 7), nums can be changed to [1, 2, 6, 10, 8].
The absolute differences between adjacent elements are:

|1 - 2| == 1
|2 - 6| == 4
|6 - 10| == 4
|10 - 8| == 2


Example 2:

Input: nums = [-1,-1,-1]
Output: 0
Explanation:
By choosing the pair as (4, 4), nums can be changed to [4, 4, 4].

Example 3:

Input: nums = [-1,10,-1,8]
Output: 1
Explanation:
By choosing the pair as (11, 9), nums can be changed to [11, 10, 9, 8].

 
Constraints:

2 <= nums.length <= 10^5
nums[i] is either -1 or in the range [1, 10^9].

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minDifference(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's derive robust check.

We can characterize each run as a constraint on S. Then need decide if there exists S={x,y} satisfying all constraints. Since number of runs O(n), constraints types.

Let's define S as unordered pair (a,b) with a<=b, positive integers, possibly equal. We need check existence.

Types:
- Type I: S intersects interval I. (boundary runs, internal k=1? Actually k=1 constraint S intersects C=A∩B, so Type I.)
- Type II: For internal run k>=2 with boundary intervals A,B: constraint is:
    (S ∩ A ∩ B ≠ ∅) OR (S ∩ A ≠ ∅ AND S ∩ B ≠ ∅ AND (if S has two distinct values then b-a <= D? Actually if no common, the two distinct values are one in A, one in B; need distance <=D. If S has common, no distance constraint on other.)
  Equivalently: There exist u,v in S (maybe same) with u∈A, v∈B, |u-v|<=D. Because if u=v, common; if distinct, distance<=D. Yes Type II is simply: ∃ u,v ∈ S such that u∈A, v∈B, |u-v|<=D. Since S has at most two values. This is a "pair of points from S hitting two intervals with distance <=D". If S has one value, need value in A∩B (distance 0). If S has two values, need one in A, one in B (or same) and distance<=D.

Type I can also be seen as ∃ u∈S with u∈I. Type II as ∃ u,v∈S with u∈A, v∈B, |u-v|<=D.

We need decide existence of at most 2 points (with repetition) satisfying a set of such constraints.

This resembles 2-dimensional? Since S has two points a,b. Constraints are on individual points (Type I) and on pairs (Type II). We can choose a,b. Need efficient O(n log n) maybe.

Maybe there is a simpler greedy for fixed D: The optimal pair can be found by considering "forbidden" intervals? Let's explore.

Since x,y are just two numbers, we can think of choosing a set S of size <=2. Type I constraints require S hits each interval. Type II constraints require there exist two (not necessarily distinct) elements in S that are within D and hit A and B. If S has two elements far apart, Type II may fail for runs that need both intervals. But if a run has a common value in A∩B, then it doesn't constrain distance.

Maybe we can reduce Type II to Type I by noting: If D is fixed and intervals A,B are length 2D. If A and B overlap, then any value in overlap hits both, so Type II is automatically satisfied if S hits A∩B. But if S doesn't hit overlap, it still may be satisfied by two values within D. However if A and B are separated by >? Let's analyze.

A=[L-D,L+D], B=[R-D,R+D]. Assume L<=R. Distance between intervals:
- If R-L <= 2D, intervals overlap; A∩B nonempty.
- If R-L > 2D, gap = R-L-2D >0.

Type II requires ∃ u∈A, v∈B with |u-v|<=D. Since A left of B (if L<=R). The minimal distance between A and B is gap = R-L-2D. Thus Type II feasible only if gap <= D, i.e., R-L <= 3D. Let's analyze run constraints more and see if they can be transformed into interval hitting with at most two points plus a distance condition.

Let S={a,b}. For each run:
- Boundary run: interval I. Constraint: a∈I or b∈I.
- Internal k=1: interval C=A∩B. Constraint: a∈C or b∈C.
- Internal k>=2: A,B. Constraint: ∃ u,v∈S with u∈A, v∈B, |u-v|<=D.
  This is equivalent to: (a∈A and a∈B) or (b∈A and b∈B) or (a∈A and b∈B and |a-b|<=D) or (a∈B and b∈A and |a-b|<=D). Since A,B intervals.

If |a-b|<=D, then Type II reduces to (a∈A or b∈A) and (a∈B or b∈B)? Wait if |a-b|<=D, and S hits both A and B, then choose u=a or b in A, v=a or b in B; distance between chosen u,v is either 0 or |a-b|<=D. So yes, if the two points are within D, Type II is equivalent to S intersects A and S intersects B. If S has one point, same. If |a-b|>D, then Type II requires either a single point in A∩B (so S intersects overlap) or? If a,b far, cannot use one in A and one in B because distance>D. So only common value in A∩B can satisfy. Thus for Type II:
- If S has two values with distance >D, then every internal run k>=2 must have S ∩ A ∩ B ≠ ∅ (i.e., one of the two values lies in the overlap of endpoint intervals). Because if no common, would need use both values with distance >D impossible.
- If S has two values with distance <=D, then Type II is just S hits A and B (not necessarily overlap).

This is interesting. Let's denote "close pair" if |a-b|<=D, "far pair" if |a-b|>D.

If S is close pair, then all constraints simplify:
- Type I: S hits interval.
- Type II: S hits A and S hits B (because close).
So global check for close pair: Need choose two points within distance D such that for every interval constraint (Type I or endpoint interval A/B from Type II) S hits it. Actually Type II with A,B requires S hits A and S hits B. Thus all constraints become a set of intervals that S must hit, plus distance between the two points <=D. If S has one point (a=b), distance 0, need one point hitting all intervals (all A,B,C,I). If S has two close points, need two points within D hitting all intervals.

If S is far pair (|a-b|>D):
- Type I: S hits interval.
- Type II: For each internal run k>=2, S must hit the overlap O=A∩B (because no close cross). Additionally, if S hits O, then Type II satisfied by that common value. Wait if S hits O, yes. It doesn't require S hits A and B separately? If value in O is in both. So Type II becomes Type I on O. If O empty, impossible for far pair. So for far pair, all constraints are just interval hitting: S must hit every interval (I, C, O). No distance constraint (other than >D, but if they hit all intervals and are far, okay; if they happen close also okay). But if S hits all intervals with two points, and the two points are far, then all internal runs use common value? Actually if S hits O for each internal run, then each run can use the value in O. If the two points are far, each run uses one of them, whichever lies in its O. Could different runs use different far values; no cross-run issue. So far pair existence reduces to: can two points hit all "required intervals" (Type I intervals and overlaps O for k>=2), with no distance constraint. If the two points happen to be close, also okay. So actually for any S, if it hits all required intervals (where for Type II required interval is O? Wait if S close, hitting A and B separately may be enough even if O not hit. But if S hits O, then it also hits A and B. If S close and hits A and B but not O, Type II satisfied. So requiring O hit is too strong for close pairs. But if we allow far pair, close pair can also be considered; maybe we can check two separate cases:
1. There exists S (any distance) hitting all intervals of form:
   - boundary runs: I
   - internal k=1: C
   - internal k>=2: O = A∩B
   If yes, then choose S; for internal k>=2, use the value in O, so Type II satisfied regardless of distance. This is sufficient but not necessary: a close pair might satisfy Type II by hitting A and B separately without hitting O. Example L=0,R=25,D=10, A=[-10,10], B=[15,35], O empty. k>=2 run. Choose S={10,15}, distance5<=D. S hits A and B, Type II satisfied, but O empty. Our "hit O" would reject. So need close case.

2. There exists S with distance <=D hitting all intervals of form:
   - Type I intervals I,C
   - For each internal k>=2, both A and B (i.e., S hits A and S hits B).
   If yes, choose S close; for TypeII use close cross. This covers close pairs. But what about far pairs that don't hit O? Far pair cannot satisfy TypeII if O empty, because would need cross distance >D. So far pairs must hit O. Thus union of:
   A) S hits all "overlap intervals" (O for k>=2, C for k=1, I for boundary) — no distance constraint. (This covers far pairs and some close.)
   B) S is close (distance<=D) and hits all "endpoint intervals" for k>=2 (A and B) plus Type I intervals. (This covers close pairs, including those not hitting O.)
Is that complete? Let's verify.

For a given S:
- For boundary and k=1 runs, constraints are Type I: S hits I/C. Both cases require hitting these intervals. In case A, include them. In case B, include them too.
- For internal k>=2:
   If S hits O, then satisfied regardless of distance. Case A.
   If S does not hit O, then to satisfy TypeII, S must have two distinct values one in A, one in B, and distance<=D. Thus S is close and hits A and B. Case B.
So any S satisfies either (for all internal runs, either hit O? Wait S may hit O for some runs and not for others. If S is far, it must hit O for all internal runs. If S is close, for runs where it doesn't hit O, it can hit A,B. Case B requires S close and hits A,B for all internal runs, which is sufficient for all internal runs regardless of O. But what if S is close and for some run hits O but not both A,B? If hits O, it hits both A,B automatically. So close S hitting A,B all runs is equivalent to close S satisfying all TypeII. Yes.
What if S is far but for some internal run hits O, for another also hits O; case A. Good.
What if S is close but not hitting A,B for all runs, yet satisfies TypeII for all runs? TypeII requires for each run either hit O (which implies hit A,B) or cross close (requires hit A,B). So it must hit A,B for each run. Thus case B necessary for close S. Good.

Thus check can be:
- If there exist two points (possibly equal) hitting all intervals in set P (overlap requirements) -> true.
- Or if there exist two points within distance D hitting all intervals in set Q (where Q includes boundary intervals, k=1 intervals, and for each internal k>=2 both A and B) -> true.

Wait case B "S close and hits all endpoint intervals A,B for internal runs" means the set Q contains A and B as separate intervals for each internal run. Since S must hit A and S must hit B. Yes.

But is case A "S hits all overlap intervals" sufficient if S has two points far and one point hits O for each internal run, but for boundary intervals maybe the other point hits; no distance constraint. For internal runs, use the point in O. If different internal runs require different points (one run O contains a, another O contains b), that's fine because each run can choose its own common value. No adjacent missing between runs. So yes.

Now we need solve two subproblems:

2. Given a set of intervals Q, can two points within distance D hit all intervals? Need decide. This is more complex. We need choose a,b positive with |a-b|<=D such that every interval contains a or b. This is equivalent to: There exists a closed interval [a,b] of length <=D (with a<=b) such that every given interval contains a or b. Actually if a<=b and b-a<=D. Need hit all intervals by endpoints of this "short segment". If a=b, one point.

How to check efficiently? This is a known problem: Given intervals, can two points with distance <=D stab all intervals. Need O(m log m). Let's derive.

Let intervals [l_i, r_i]. Need choose a<=b, b-a<=D, and for each i: l_i <= a <= r_i OR l_i <= b <= r_i.

Equivalently, no interval is completely to the right of b (l_i > b) or completely to the left of a (r_i < a). Since a<=b.

For a fixed a, what b values work? Need b ∈ [a, a+D] and:
- For intervals with r_i < a: they are not hit by a, so must be hit by b: require l_i <= b <= r_i. Since r_i < a <= b, b<=r_i impossible. Wait if r_i < a, then b >= a > r_i, so b cannot be in interval. Thus any interval entirely left of a makes impossible. Therefore a must be <= r_i for all intervals? Actually if r_i < a, interval lies left of a; neither a nor b (b>=a) can be in it. So impossible. Thus a <= min r_i? More precisely a <= r_i for all i, so a <= R_min = min r_i.

- For intervals with l_i <= a <= r_i, hit by a. For intervals with l_i <= b <= r_i, hit by b. Intervals that contain neither endpoint cause fail.

If a <= all r_i and b >= all l_i, then any interval? Wait an interval [l,r] could have r < a? no because a<=r. Could have l > b? no because b>=l. Could have l > a and r < b (interval strictly between a and b): then neither endpoint in interval, fail. Example interval [5,6], a=0,b=10,D=10: a<=r, b>=l, but neither 0 nor10 in [5,6]. For each interval, condition is (a∈[l,r]) OR (b∈[l,r]). Since a<=b. This is equivalent to not (r < a OR l > b), i.e., a <= r AND b >= l. Wait for interval [5,6], a=0<=6 true, b=10>=5 true, so condition would be true by this logic, but actually neither endpoint in interval. If a<l and b>r, then both fail. This corresponds to interval strictly between a and b. So need also not (a < l AND b > r). Thus condition: a <= r, b >= l, and (a >= l OR b <= r). Equivalent to intervals cannot be strictly inside (a,b).

So for fixed a, b must satisfy:
- b ∈ [a, a+D]
- For each interval:
   * If r_i < a: impossible (as above)
   * If l_i > b: impossible
   * If l_i > a and r_i < b: impossible (interval strictly between)
Actually if l_i > a and r_i < b, then neither endpoint. If l_i <= a, a hits. If r_i >= b, b hits. If l_i <= a <= r_i or l_i <= b <= r_i.

We can characterize feasible (a,b) as:
- a <= min_i r_i? Because if a > r_i, interval left of a impossible. Yes a <= R_min.
- b >= max_i l_i? Because if b < l_i, interval right of b impossible. Yes b >= L_max.
- For every interval, not (a < l_i and b > r_i). That is, no interval lies completely between a and b. Equivalently, for all i, a >= l_i OR b <= r_i.

Thus feasible if:
a <= R_min,
b >= L_max,
b - a <= D,
and for all i: (a >= l_i) OR (b <= r_i).

This is promising. The last condition says every interval must have its left endpoint <= a OR its right endpoint >= b. In other words, intervals that start after a must end before b? Wait if a < l_i, then to avoid interval between, need b <= r_i. So for intervals with l_i > a, we need b <= r_i. Thus b <= min_{i: l_i > a} r_i. If no such intervals, no upper bound from this. Also b <= a+D. Additionally b >= L_max. And a <= R_min.

- Let Lmax = max l_i.

Now need implement interval hitting by at most two points (no distance constraint). Classic greedy.

Given intervals [l,r] (positive integers, nonempty). Need decide if there exist two points p1<=p2 such that each interval contains p1 or p2. Since points can be equal, at most two.

Classic algorithm:
- Sort intervals by left endpoint? Actually for minimum points to stab intervals, greedy: sort by right endpoint ascending; pick point at first right endpoint, remove all intervals containing it; if remaining nonempty, pick point at next interval's right endpoint; if all covered true. This yields minimum number of points. Since intervals closed, point at r covers intervals with l<=r<=r? Wait if sorted by r, choose r of first interval. It covers all intervals with l<=r. Because first interval has smallest r among remaining; any interval with l<=r is covered by point r. Intervals with l>r remain. Then repeat. This gives min points. Need at most 2. We can implement:
```
def can_hit_two(intervals):
    if not intervals: return True
    # clip already
    sort by r
    points=0
    i=0
    while i < len(intervals):
       r = intervals[i][1]
       points += 1
       if points > 2: return False
       while i < len and intervals[i][0] <= r:
           i += 1
    return True
```
But is sorting by r enough if intervals with same r? Yes. Need also intervals with l>r none. This greedy chooses rightmost possible point? Actually choosing r of first interval is optimal because any point covering first interval must be <=r and >=l; choosing r maximizes coverage of later intervals. Good.

Simpler: Iterate over nums and identify runs of -1:
```
i=0
n=len(nums)
while i<n:
  if nums[i] != -1:
      i+=1; continue
  start=i
  while i<n and nums[i]==-1: i+=1
  # run [start, i-1]
  left_known = nums[start-1] if start>0 else None
  right_known = nums[i] if i<n else None
  process run
```

```
known_idx = [i for i,v in enumerate(nums) if v!=-1]
if not known_idx: return 0

    if b == a+1:
        if abs(nums[a]-nums[b]) > D: return False
    else:

# boundary runs:

```

Now, let's derive interval functions.

```
def interval(v):
    return (max(1, v-D), v+D)
```
Since v+D >=1. If D maybe 0.

Intersection:
```
def intersect(I,J):
    l=max(I[0],J[0]); r=min(I[1],J[1])
    if l<=r: return (l,r)
    else: None
```

In check(D):
```
def check(D):
    # known-known adjacent
    for i in range(n-1):
        if nums[i] != -1 and nums[i+1] != -1:
            if abs(nums[i]-nums[i+1]) > D:
                return False
    # But if we later process runs, this loop O(n) okay. Could combine with run scan.
```
Need be careful: If we process runs by known indices, known-known adjacent only between consecutive known indices with no missing. We can just loop over all adjacent known indices from known_idx list:
```
for a,b in zip(known_idx, known_idx[1:]):
    if b == a+1:
        if abs(nums[a]-nums[b]) > D: return False
```
This is O(#known). Good. But if there are known-known adjacent pairs not consecutive known? Consecutive known indices are exactly adjacent known values; if b>a+1, there is missing run. Good.

Then process runs:
```
P=[]; Q=[]
p_empty=False
# left boundary
first=known_idx[0]
if first > 0:
    I = interval(nums[first])
    P.append(I); Q.append(I)
# between knowns
for a,b in zip(known_idx, known_idx[1:]):
    if b == a+1:
        continue # checked
    k = b - a - 1
    A = interval(nums[a])
    B = interval(nums[b])
    if k == 1:
        C = intersect(A,B)
        if C is None:
            return False
        P.append(C); Q.append(C)
    else: # k>=2
        O = intersect(A,B)
        if O is not None:
            P.append(O)
        else:
            p_empty = True
        Q.append(A); Q.append(B)
# right boundary
last=known_idx[-1]
if last < n-1:
    I = interval(nums[last])
    P.append(I); Q.append(I)
```

Now, can_hit_two(P) with p_empty false. Implementation:
```
def can_hit_two(intervals):
    if not intervals:
        return True
    r1 = min(r for l,r in intervals)
    # remove intervals with l <= r1
    max_l_rem = -inf
    min_r_rem = inf
    any_rem = False
    for l,r in intervals:
        if l > r1:
            any_rem = True
            if l > max_l_rem: max_l_rem = l
            if r < min_r_rem: min_r_rem = r
    if not any_rem:
        return True
    return max_l_rem <= min_r_rem
```
Is this always equivalent to greedy? Let's prove with intervals sorted by r, first point r1=min r. Remove intervals with l<=r1. Remaining intervals all have l>r1. Need one point to stab remaining iff max_l_rem <= min_r_rem. Yes. But wait what if an interval with l<=r1 but r < r1? impossible since r1 is min r, so no. Good.
But is choosing r1=min r always optimal for two points? Suppose there is an interval with min r, but maybe choose first point less than r1 to leave room for second? Since first point must be in that interval, it can be any p in [l_min_r, r1]. Choosing p=r1 maximizes coverage of intervals with l<=r1; intervals with l>r1 are unaffected (p<r1 also not cover them). Could choosing p<r1 cover some interval with l between p and r1 that p=r1 also covers? p=r1 covers all l<=r1, so yes. Thus p=r1 dominates any p<r1. Good.

Now, can_hit_two_close(Q,D):
```
def can_hit_two_close(intervals, D):
    if not intervals:
        return True
    Lmax = max(l for l,r in intervals)
    Rmin = min(r for l,r in intervals)
    if Lmax - Rmin > D:
        return False
    T = -10**30
    for l,r in intervals:
        if r < Lmax:
            if l > T: T = l
    a = max(1, Lmax - D, T)
    return a <= Rmin
```
Need verify with intervals where T maybe > Lmax? Since if r < Lmax, then l <= r < Lmax, so l < Lmax. So T < Lmax. Good.
Need also consider if a maybe > b? b = max(Lmax,a). If a<=Rmin and Lmax-Rmin<=D, then Lmax <= Rmin+D. Since a>=Lmax-D, Lmax <= a+D. If a>Lmax, b=a, distance0. Good.
Need ensure a positive. If Lmax-D could be negative; max with1. But what if a must also be <= some interval's right endpoint? Rmin ensures a<=all r. Good.
What if intervals have l<1? We clipped, so l>=1. Good.

Let's attempt to prove close check formula in more detail to ensure no hidden flaw.

We have intervals Q. Need ∃ a<=b, b-a<=D, ∀i: a∈I_i or b∈I_i.

Let L = max l_i, R = min r_i.

Necessary:
- a <= R: if a > R, let interval with r=R. Since b>=a>R, neither a nor b in [l,R] (a>R, b>R). fail.
- b >= L: if b < L, interval with l=L. Since a<=b<L, neither in. fail.
- Therefore b-a >= L-R, so L-R <= D necessary.

Now, suppose L-R <= D. Need choose a. For intervals with l_i <= a, if also a<=r_i (guaranteed by a<=R<=r_i), then a hits. For intervals with l_i > a, a doesn't hit; b must hit, so need b <= r_i for all such intervals. Thus b <= U(a) = min_{l_i>a} r_i. Also b >= L (from above) and b >= a. We also need b <= a+D. So feasible iff ∃a with:
a <= R,
max(L,a) <= min(a+D, U(a)).
Since U(a)>a for any interval with l>a? If l>a, r>=l>a, so U(a)>a if finite. Thus a <= U(a) automatically. So conditions reduce to:
a <= R,
L <= a+D (i.e., a >= L-D),
L <= U(a).
Also a positive. Is max(L,a) <= a+D always? If L <= a+D yes; if a maybe >L, a<=a+D. Good.
Is max(L,a) <= U(a) iff L<=U(a) and a<=U(a). a<=U(a) automatic. So yes.
Thus need a ∈ [max(1,L-D), R] and U(a) >= L.

Now, U(a) >= L iff there is no interval with l>a and r<L. Let T = max{l_i | r_i < L}. If no such, T=-∞. For any a >= T, no bad interval has l>a? Suppose bad interval with r<L and l>T? T is max l, so l<=T<=a, so not l>a. Thus U(a)>=L. If a < T, then the bad interval with l=T has l>a and r<L, so U(a)<L. Thus condition U(a)>=L iff a >= T. Therefore feasible iff max(1,L-D,T) <= R. This is exactly our check. Good.

Need also consider if intervals can have l_i > r_i? We ensure nonempty.

Now, can_hit_two(P) proof:
Need ∃ at most two points hitting all intervals. Greedy: choose first point at R1 = min r. Any feasible solution's first point (leftmost) must be <= R1 and in the interval with min r; choosing R1 instead cannot hurt because it covers every interval whose left endpoint <= R1, and intervals with left endpoint >R1 are not covered by any point <=R1 (so need second point anyway). After removing intervals with l<=R1, remaining intervals all have l>R1. They must be hit by one point iff max l <= min r. This is necessary and sufficient. Good.

Now, let's think about interval sets P and Q in relation to p_empty. Suppose P has empty O for one run, p_empty True, can_hit_two(P) returns False. But what if P also has other intervals and can_hit_two would be false anyway; okay. We skip adding empty. If p_empty True, no point can hit all P because empty interval cannot be hit. So can_hit_two false. Good.

Now, let's consider if internal k>=2 and O nonempty but P includes O. If P can be hit by two points, case A true. But what if the two points hitting P are not positive? We clip intervals to positive, so points chosen positive. Good.

Now, let's think about performance. check(D) loops over known_idx and runs. known_idx length up to n. For each check, we build P,Q lists. In worst case, Q length up to 2*runs + boundaries, O(n). P length O(runs). can_hit_two and close each scan lists. So O(n) per check. Binary search ~31 => O(n log V) ~3.1M iterations, plus list allocations. Good.

But wait if we scan known_idx and for each pair with missing run, we append to Q. If there are many adjacent known pairs (no missing), we just check diff. If all known, Q empty, P empty, check O(n). Good.

Potential optimization: We can combine known-known check with run processing in one loop over known_idx. We already do.

Let's write code carefully.

Pseudo:

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minDifference(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        known = [i for i, v in enumerate(nums) if v != -1]
        if not known:
            return 0

        def can_two(intervals):
            if not intervals:
                return True
            r1 = min(r for _, r in intervals)
            max_l = -10**30
            min_r = 10**30
            has = False
            for l, r in intervals:
                if l > r1:
                    has = True
                    if l > max_l: max_l = l
                    if r < min_r: min_r = r
            if not has:
                return True
            return max_l <= min_r

        def can_close(intervals, D):
            if not intervals:
                return True
            Lmax = max(l for l, _ in intervals)
            Rmin = min(r for _, r in intervals)
            if Lmax - Rmin > D:
                return False
            T = -10**30
            for l, r in intervals:
                if r < Lmax:
                    if l > T:
                        T = l
            a = max(1, Lmax - D, T)
            return a <= Rmin

        def check(D):
            P = []
            Q = []
            p_empty = False

            first = known[0]
            if first > 0:
                v = nums[first]
                I = (max(1, v - D), v + D)
                P.append(I)
                Q.append(I)

            for idx in range(len(known)-1):
                a_idx = known[idx]
                b_idx = known[idx+1]
                if b_idx == a_idx + 1:
                    if abs(nums[a_idx] - nums[b_idx]) > D:
                        return False
                else:
                    k = b_idx - a_idx - 1
                    A = (max(1, nums[a_idx] - D), nums[a_idx] + D)
                    B = (max(1, nums[b_idx] - D), nums[b_idx] + D)
                    if k == 1:
                        l = max(A[0], B[0])
                        r = min(A[1], B[1])
                        if l > r:
                            return False
                        C = (l, r)
                        P.append(C)
                        Q.append(C)
                    else:
                        l = max(A[0], B[0])
                        r = min(A[1], B[1])
                        if l <= r:
                            P.append((l, r))
                        else:
                            p_empty = True
                        Q.append(A)
                        Q.append(B)

            last = known[-1]
            if last < n - 1:
                v = nums[last]
                I = (max(1, v - D), v + D)
                P.append(I)
                Q.append(I)

            if not p_empty and can_two(P):
                return True
            if can_close(Q, D):
                return True
            return False

        lo, hi = 0, 10**9
        while lo < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            if check(mid):
                hi = mid
            else:
                lo = mid + 1
        return lo
```

Need ensure `known` list built once outside check. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a missing run between knowns with k>=2 and O empty but Q close true, and if run length k=2, and the point in A is a, point in B is b, but a,b maybe not within D? S close ensures.

Now, let's think about if there is a missing run between knowns with k>=2 and O empty but Q close true,
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minDifference(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        known = [i for i, v in enumerate(nums) if v != -1]

        if not known:
            return 0

        def can_two_points(intervals):
            """Can at most two positive integer points hit all intervals?"""
            if not intervals:
                return True

            r1 = min(r for _, r in intervals)

            max_l = -10**30
            min_r = 10**30
            has_remaining = False

            for l, r in intervals:
                if l > r1:
                    has_remaining = True
                    if l > max_l:
                        max_l = l
                    if r < min_r:
                        min_r = r

            if not has_remaining:
                return True

            return max_l <= min_r

        def can_two_close_points(intervals, D):
            """Can two positive integer points with distance <= D hit all intervals?"""
            if not intervals:
                return True

            Lmax = max(l for l, _ in intervals)
            Rmin = min(r for _, r in intervals)

            if Lmax - Rmin > D:
                return False

            T = -10**30
            for l, r in intervals:
                if r < Lmax:
                    if l > T:
                        T = l

            a = max(1, Lmax - D, T)
            return a <= Rmin

        def check(D):
            P = []  # intervals for the "use one common value per run" case
            Q = []  # intervals for the "two close values" case
            p_has_empty = False

            # Left boundary run
            first = known[0]
            if first > 0:
                v = nums[first]
                interval = (max(1, v - D), v + D)
                P.append(interval)
                Q.append(interval)

            # Runs between known values
            for idx in range(len(known) - 1):
                a_idx = known[idx]
                b_idx = known[idx + 1]

                if b_idx == a_idx + 1:
                    if abs(nums[a_idx] - nums[b_idx]) > D:
                        return False
                    continue

                k = b_idx - a_idx - 1
                A = (max(1, nums[a_idx] - D), nums[a_idx] + D)
                B = (max(1, nums[b_idx] - D), nums[b_idx] + D)

                if k == 1:
                    l = max(A[0], B[0])
                    r = min(A[1], B[1])
                    if l > r:
                        return False
                    C = (l, r)
                    P.append(C)
                    Q.append(C)
                else:
                    l = max(A[0], B[0])
                    r = min(A[1], B[1])
                    if l <= r:
                        P.append((l, r))
                    else:
                        p_has_empty = True

                    Q.append(A)
                    Q.append(B)

            # Right boundary run
            last = known[-1]
            if last < n - 1:
                v = nums[last]
                interval = (max(1, v - D), v + D)
                P.append(interval)
                Q.append(interval)

            if not p_has_empty and can_two_points(P):
                return True

            if can_two_close_points(Q, D):
                return True

            return False

        lo, hi = 0, 10**9
        while lo < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            if check(mid):
                hi = mid
            else:
                lo = mid + 1

        return lo
```
